- **Morning ride cost per day:** \$6
- **Afternoon ride cost per day:** \$2
- **Total cost per day:** \$6 + \$2 = \$8

Since he drives twice a day for 14 days:
- **Total cost:** \$8/day × 14 days = \$112

Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Number of dogs**: 10
2. **Time per dog per day**: 0.5 hours

\[

\]

Now, there are 7 days in a week. So, the total time per week is:
\[

\]

Let's determine how many centimeters shorter the candle will be after burning from 1:00 PM to 5:00 PM.

1. **Time Duration:**
   - From 1:00 PM to 5:00 PM is 4 hours.

2. **Melting Rate:**
   - The candle melts at a rate of 2 centimeters per hour.

3. **Total Melting:**
   - Multiply the number of hours by the melting rate:
     \[
     4 \text{ hours} \times 2 \text{ centimeters/hour} = 8 \text{ centimeters}
     \]

Therefore, the candle will be **8 centimeters shorter** after burning from 1:00 PM to 5:00 PM.

To determine how many feet of crepe paper James needs to buy to hang streamers from his backyard fence, we need to find the perimeter of the rectangular fence. The perimeter of a rectangle is calculated as:

\[
\text{Perimeter} = 2 \times (\text{length} + \text{width})
\]

Given:
- Length (long side) = 20 feet
- Width (short side) = 15 feet

Plug in the values:

\[
\text{Perimeter} = 2 \times (20 + 15) = 2 \times 35 = 70 \text{ feet}
\]

Therefore, James needs to buy **70 feet** of crepe paper.

Let's calculate the time it takes to ride each slide and then find the difference.

1. **Biggest slide:**
   - Length: 300 feet
   - Speed: 60 feet/minute
   - Time = Distance / Speed = \( \frac{300}{60} = 5 \) minutes.

2. **Second biggest slide:**
   - Length: 240 feet
   - Speed: 80 feet/minute
   - Time = Distance / Speed = \( \frac{240}{80} = 3 \) minutes.

3. **Difference in time:**
   - Time on biggest slide - Time on second biggest slide = \( 5 - 3 = 2 \) minutes.

**Therefore, it takes 2 minutes longer to ride the biggest slide compared to the second biggest slide.**

Let's break down the cost over the 2 years (24 months) step by step:

1. **First 6 months**: $8 per month  
   Cost = 6 × $8 = $48

2. **Next 8 months**: $12 per month (normal price)  
   Cost = 8 × $12 = $96

3. **Remaining months**:  
   Total months so far = 6 + 8 = 14 months  
   Months left in 2 years = 24 - 14 = 10 months  
   New price = $14 per month  
   Cost = 10 × $14 = $140

**Total cost**:  
$48 (first 6) + $96 (next 8) + $140 (last 10) = $284

So, 2 years of the service cost Bill **$284**.

Let's break down the problem step by step:

2. **Daily Expense**: She buys 4 lollipops each day, each costing 25 cents.
   - Cost per lollipop = 25 cents
   - Total cost for 4 lollipops = 4 × 25 = 100 cents
   - Since 100 cents = \$1, she spends \$1 each day.

   - Pocket money = \$5
   - Expense = \$1
   - Savings per day = \$5 - \$1 = \$4

   - Savings per day = \$4
   - Total savings in 5 days = 5 × \$4 = \$20

Therefore, Marisa puts **\$20** in her piggy bank after 5 days.

Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Current Milk Production:**
   - The farmer has 3 cows.
   - Each cow produces 5 liters of milk per day.
   - So, current total milk production = 3 cows × 5 liters/cow/day = **15 liters per day**.

2. **Desired Milk Production:**
   - The farmer wants to produce **25 liters per day**.

3. **Additional Milk Needed:**
   - Additional milk required = Desired production - Current production = 25 liters - 15 liters = **10 liters per day**.

4. **Cows Needed for Additional Milk:**
   - Each cow produces 5 liters per day.
   - Number of additional cows needed = Additional milk required ÷ Milk per cow = 10 liters ÷ 5 liters/cow = **2 cows**.

**Therefore, the farmer needs 2 more cows to produce 25 liters of milk per day.**

Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given:**
- In 10 years, Melanie will be 18 years old.

Let Melanie's present age be \( x \) years.

So, in 10 years:
\[
x + 10 = 18
\]
Solving for \( x \):
\[
x = 18 - 10 = 8
\]
So, Melanie is currently 8 years old.

**We need to find:** In how many years (let that be \( y \) years) will her age be thrice her present age?

Thrice her present age is \( 3 \times 8 = 24 \) years.

So, in \( y \) years:
\[
8 + y = 24
\]
Solving for \( y \):
\[
y = 24 - 8 = 16
\]

**Therefore, in 16 years, Melanie will be 24 years old, which is thrice her present age (8 years).**

\[
\boxed{16}
\]

1. **Daily Activity:**
   - Jim watches TV for 2 hours.
   - Then, he reads for half as long as he watched TV. So, reading time = \( \frac{1}{2} \times 2 = 1 \) hour.
   - Therefore, each day he spends \( 2 \) hours (TV) + \( 1 \) hour (reading) = \( 3 \) hours total.

2. **Weekly Frequency:**
   - He does this 3 times a week.
   - So, weekly total = \( 3 \) days/week \( \times 3 \) hours/day = \( 9 \) hours.

   - Total time = \( 4 \) weeks \( \times 9 \) hours/week = \( 36 \) hours.

**Final Answer:**

Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Define the variables:**
   - Let \( c \) be the width of the chair.
   - The rug is 5 feet wider than the chair, so the width of the rug \( r = c + 5 \).
   - The couch is 2 feet longer than twice the width of the rug, so the length of the couch \( s = 2r + 2 \).

2. **Given:**
   - The chair is 3 feet wide, so \( c = 3 \).

3. **Calculate the width of the rug:**
   - \( r = c + 5 = 3 + 5 = 8 \) feet.

4. **Calculate the length of the couch:**
   - \( s = 2r + 2 = 2(8) + 2 = 16 + 2 = 18 \) feet.

**Therefore, the couch is 18 feet long.**

\[
\boxed{18}
\]

Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Given:**
   - Internet connection speed: 20 kb per second.
   - 1 Mb = 1000 kb.

2. **We need to find the speed in Mb per hour.**

3. **Convert kb per second to kb per hour:**
   - There are 60 seconds in a minute and 60 minutes in an hour.
   - So, 1 hour = 60 × 60 = 3600 seconds.
   - Therefore, speed in kb per hour = 20 kb/s × 3600 s = 72,000 kb/hour.

4. **Convert kb per hour to Mb per hour:**
   - Since 1 Mb = 1000 kb, we divide by 1000.
   - Speed in Mb per hour = 72,000 / 1000 = 72 Mb/hour.

5. **Final Answer:**
   \[
   \boxed{72}
   \]
   Ashley's internet connection speed is 72 Mb per hour.

Let's determine the initial amount of meat on the triceratops step by step.

Let \( x \) be the initial amount of meat in kilograms.

1. **T-Rex ate half:**
   - Amount eaten: \( \frac{1}{2}x \)
   - Remaining: \( x - \frac{1}{2}x = \frac{1}{2}x \)

2. **Velociraptors scavenged half of what was left:**
   - They ate: \( \frac{1}{2} \times \frac{1}{2}x = \frac{1}{4}x \)
   - Remaining after raptors: \( \frac{1}{2}x - \frac{1}{4}x = \frac{1}{4}x \)

3. **Allosaurus ate the last 270 kg:**
   - This remaining amount is \( \frac{1}{4}x \), which equals 270 kg.

So,
\[
\frac{1}{4}x = 270
\]

Solving for \( x \):
\[
x = 270 \times 4 = 1080
\]

**Therefore, the triceratops initially had 1080 kilograms of meat.**

\[
\boxed{1080}
\]

Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Initial number of cards**:  
   Elaine initially had **20** Pokemon cards.

2. **After the first month**:  
   She collected three times the initial number.  
   So, cards collected in the first month = \(3 \times 20 = 60\).  
   Total after first month = initial + first month collection = \(20 + 60 = 80\).

3. **After the second month**:  
   She collected 20 fewer cards than she collected in the first month.  
   So, cards collected in the second month = \(60 - 20 = 40\).  
   Total after second month = previous total + second month collection = \(80 + 40 = 120\).

4. **After the third month**:  
   She collected twice the combined number of cards she collected in the first and second months.  
   Combined from first and second months = \(60 + 40 = 100\).  
   So, cards collected in the third month = \(2 \times 100 = 200\).  
   Total after third month = previous total + third month collection = \(120 + 200 = 320\).

Therefore, Elaine now has **320** Pokemon cards in total.

\boxed{320}

Token usage: 83 input + 268 output = 351 total
Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Define Variables:**
   - Let \( c \) be the number of cupcakes.
   - Then, the number of brownies is \( 3c - 2 \) (since it's "2 less than three times as many brownies as cupcakes").

2. **Total Treats:**
   - There are 5 people, and each gets 2 treats.
   - So, total treats = \( 5 \times 2 = 10 \).

3. **Set Up the Equation:**
   - Total treats = cupcakes + brownies = \( c + (3c - 2) = 10 \).
   - Simplify: \( c + 3c - 2 = 10 \) → \( 4c - 2 = 10 \).

4. **Solve for \( c \):**
   - Add 2 to both sides: \( 4c = 12 \).
   - Divide both sides by 4: \( c = 3 \).

5. **Verification:**
   - Cupcakes: \( c = 3 \).
   - Brownies: \( 3 \times 3 - 2 = 9 - 2 = 7 \).
   - Total treats: \( 3 + 7 = 10 \), which matches the required total.

**Final Answer:**
\[

Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Let:**
- \( J \) be Jame's current age.
- \( C \) be his cousin's current age.

**Given:**
1. Jame will turn 27 in 5 years.
   - So, \( J + 5 = 27 \)
   - Therefore, \( J = 27 - 5 = 22 \)

2. In 8 years, his cousin will be 5 years younger than twice Jame's age (at that time).
   - In 8 years:
     - Jame's age: \( J + 8 \)
     - Cousin's age: \( C + 8 \)
   - According to the problem:
        \( C + 8 = 2 \times (J + 8) - 5 \)

**Substitute \( J = 22 \) into the equation:**
\[
C + 8 = 2 \times (22 + 8) - 5
\]
\[
C + 8 = 2 \times 30 - 5
\]
\[
C + 8 = 60 - 5
\]
\[
C + 8 = 55
\]
\[
C = 55 - 8 = 47
\]

So, the cousin is currently 47 years old.

**The age difference now:**
\[
\text{Difference} = C - J = 47 - 22 = 25
\]

**Therefore, there are 25 years separating their ages now.**

\[
\boxed{25}
\]

   \[

   \]
   So, he gets \( x \) dollars from selling the cards.

   \[

   \]

5. Thus, the total number of cards he started with is \( x = 150 \).

**Verification:**

- Keeps \( \frac{1}{3} \times 150 = 50 \) cards.
- Sells \( \frac{2}{3} \times 150 = 100 \) cards.

Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Initial Plan:**
   - The caterer was told to prepare for 36 guests.
   - Most would eat one hot dog, but he prepared enough for half of the guests to have two.
   - So, half of 36 is 18 guests who could have two hot dogs.
   - Therefore, the total number of hot dogs prepared is:
        - 18 guests × 2 hot dogs = 36 hot dogs
        - The remaining 18 guests × 1 hot dog = 18 hot dogs
     But wait, if he prepared for half to have two, that means he made extra for half. Actually, he prepared enough so that half the guests could have two. So the total hot dogs prepared should be:
        - For the 36 guests: if all had one, that would be 36, but he added an extra for half, so total = 36 + 18 = 54 hot dogs.

   Alternatively, think: he prepared for 36 guests, but with the expectation that half (18) would have two. So the total hot dogs = (36 * 1) + (18 * 1 extra) = 36 + 18 = 54.

2. **What Actually Happened:**
   - 40 guests showed up (instead of 36).
   - Everyone wanted a second hot dog.

3. **How Many Hot Dogs Available:**
   - The caterer prepared 54 hot dogs.

4. **How Many Hot Dogs Needed:**
   - For 40 guests, each wanting two hot dogs: 40 × 2 = 80 hot dogs needed.

5. **Shortage:**
   - Available: 54 hot dogs
   - Needed: 80 hot dogs
   - Shortage = 80 - 54 = 26 hot dogs.

   This means 26 hot dogs are missing for seconds. Since each second hot dog serves one guest, **26 guests did not get a second hot dog**.

6. **Verification:**
   - With 54 hot dogs, we can serve:
        - 40 guests each get one hot dog (uses 40).
        - Then, only 14 hot dogs left for seconds (54 - 40 = 14).
   - So, only 14 guests get a second hot dog.
   - Therefore, the number of guests who did not get a second hot dog is 40 - 14 = 26.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{26}
\]
26 guests did not get a second hotdog.
Token usage: 78 input + 539 output = 617 total
Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Convert all measurements to the same unit:**
   - Flower bed length: 111 feet
   - Plant width: 12 inches = 1 foot
   - Spacing between plants: 1.5 feet

2. **Understand the arrangement:**
   - Each plant occupies 1 foot in width, and there is 1.5 feet of space between each plant.
   - However, at the ends of the flower bed, there might be no space needed beyond the last plant. So, the pattern is: plant, then space, then plant, and so on.

3. **Calculate the space required per plant (including spacing):**
   - For each plant (except the last one), we need to account for the plant itself (1 foot) and the spacing after it (1.5 feet). So, per plant, we effectively use 1 + 1.5 = 2.5 feet.
   - But for the last plant, we only need the plant width (1 foot) without any trailing space.

4. **Let \( n \) be the number of plants.**
   - The total length used by \( n \) plants is: 
        (number of gaps) * (spacing) + (number of plants) * (plant width)
   - There are \( n-1 \) gaps between \( n \) plants.
   - So, total length = \( (n-1) \times 1.5 + n \times 1 \)

5. **Set up the equation to find how many plants fit:**
   \[
   (n-1) \times 1.5 + n \times 1 = 111
   \]
   Simplify:
   \[
   1.5n - 1.5 + n = 111
   \]
   \[
   2.5n - 1.5 = 111
   \]
   \[
   2.5n = 111 + 1.5 = 112.5
   \]
   \[
   n = \frac{112.5}{2.5} = 45
   \]
   So, the flower bed can hold 45 plants.

6. **Pat already has 17 plants, so she needs to buy:**
   \[
   45 - 17 = 28 \text{ plants}
   \]

7. **Each plant costs $6, so total cost:**
   \[
   28 \times 6 = 168
   \]

**Therefore, Pat will spend \(\boxed{168}\) dollars at the store.**

Token usage: 64 input + 166 output = 230 total
Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Westward Journey:**
   - Elvis drives west for 5 hours at 18 mph.
   - Distance traveled west: \( \text{distance} = \text{speed} \times \text{time} = 18 \, \text{mph} \times 5 \, \text{hours} = 90 \, \text{miles} \).

2. **Eastward Journey:**
   - He then turns around and drives east for 8 hours at 18 mph.
   - Distance traveled east: \( 18 \, \text{mph} \times 8 \, \text{hours} = 144 \, \text{miles} \).

3. **Net Displacement:**
   - Since west and east are opposite directions, we can assign west as positive and east as negative (or vice versa). Let's assign west as positive and east as negative.
   - Initial displacement (west): \( +90 \) miles.
   - Then displacement (east): \( -144 \) miles.
   - Net displacement: \( +90 + (-144) = -54 \) miles.

   The negative sign indicates that he is 54 miles east of his starting point.

4. **Final Answer:**
   - Therefore, Elvis is \( \boxed{54} \) miles east of his house.

**Summary:**  
After traveling west 90 miles and then east 144 miles, the net displacement is 54 miles to the east. So, he is 54 miles from his house.
Token usage: 62 input + 322 output = 384 total
Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's determine the selling price per watermelon step by step.

1. **Cost Price (CP) for 50 watermelons:** \$80  
   So, the cost price per watermelon is:  
   \[
   \text{CP per watermelon} = \frac{80}{50} = \$1.60
   \]

2. **Profit Percentage:** 25%  
   This means the profit is 25% of the cost price.

3. **Profit per watermelon:**  
   \[
   \text{Profit per watermelon} = 25\% \text{ of } 1.60 = \frac{25}{100} \times 1.60 = 0.25 \times 1.60 = \$0.40
   \]

4. **Selling Price (SP) per watermelon:**  
   \[
   \text{SP per watermelon} = \text{CP per watermelon} + \text{Profit per watermelon} = 1.60 + 0.40 = \$2.00
   \]

Alternatively, you can calculate the selling price directly using the profit percentage:
\[
\text{SP} = \text{CP} \times \left(1 + \frac{\text{Profit\%}}{100}\right) = 1.60 \times (1 + 0.25) = 1.60 \times 1.25 = \$2.00
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{2.00}
\]
Each watermelon was sold for \$2.00.

Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's break down the TV watching time for each day:

- **Monday**: 1 hour (1 episode of 1-hour show)
- **Tuesday**: 1 hour (1 episode of 1-hour show)
- **Wednesday**: Let \( x \) be the number of 30-minute episodes. Each is 0.5 hours, so total time = \( 0.5x \) hours.
- **Thursday**: 1 hour (1 episode of 1-hour show) + 0.5 hours (1 episode of 30-minute show) = 1.5 hours
- **Friday**: 2 hours (two 1-hour episodes)

Total TV time for the week is 7 hours.

So, we can write the equation:
\[
\text{Monday} + \text{Tuesday} + \text{Wednesday} + \text{Thursday} + \text{Friday} = 7
\]
\[
1 + 1 + 0.5x + 1.5 + 2 = 7
\]

Combine the known hours:
\[
1 + 1 + 1.5 + 2 = 5.5
\]
So,
\[
5.5 + 0.5x = 7
\]

Subtract 5.5 from both sides:
\[
0.5x = 7 - 5.5
\]
\[
0.5x = 1.5
\]

Now, solve for \( x \):
\[
x = \frac{1.5}{0.5} = 3
\]

Therefore, Frankie watched **3** 30-minute episodes on Wednesday.

\boxed{3}

1. **Initial number of trees**: 50

   - Tom plants 10 trees.
   - He chops down 2 trees.
   - So, **net gain per year**: 10 planted - 2 chopped = 8 trees.

3. **After 10 years**:
   - Total net gain from planting and chopping: 10 years × 8 trees/year = 80 trees.
   - Therefore, total trees after 10 years (before the die-off): Initial trees + net gain = 50 + 80 = 130 trees.

4. **30% of the trees die**:
   - Number of trees that die: 30% of 130 = 0.30 × 130 = 39 trees.
   - Trees left: 130 - 39 = 91 trees.

**Final Answer**:
\[
\boxed{91}
\]

Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Outbound Journey (from 1 PM to 4 PM):**
   - Speed: 10 mph
   - Time: From 1 PM to 4 PM is 3 hours.
   - Distance traveled: \( \text{Distance} = \text{Speed} \times \text{Time} = 10 \, \text{mph} \times 3 \, \text{hours} = 30 \, \text{miles} \).

2. **Return Journey:**
   - Distance to cover: 30 miles (same as outbound).
   - Speed: 6 mph.
   - Time taken: \( \text{Time} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Speed}} = \frac{30 \, \text{miles}}{6 \, \text{mph}} = 5 \, \text{hours} \).

**Therefore, it takes Tom 5 hours to get back.**

\[
\boxed{5}

Token usage: 100 input + 350 output = 450 total
Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Problem Statement:**
"Eight more than four times the number of coffee mugs in the teacher’s lounge is 40."

Let \( m \) be the number of coffee mugs.

Translate the words into an equation:
- "Four times the number of coffee mugs" is \( 4m \).
- "Eight more than" that is \( 4m + 8 \).
- This equals 40.

So, the equation is:
\[
4m + 8 = 40
\]

Now, solve for \( m \):

1. Subtract 8 from both sides:
\[
4m + 8 - 8 = 40 - 8
\]
\[
4m = 32
\]

2. Divide both sides by 4:
\[
\frac{4m}{4} = \frac{32}{4}
\]
\[
m = 8
\]

**Answer:**
\[
\boxed{8}
\]
There are 8 coffee mugs in the teacher’s lounge.

   - So, her daily earnings (without the attendance bonus) are:  
     \( 7.5 \times 1.5 = 11.25 \) dollars per day.

2. **Weekly Earnings (without bonus):**
   - She works 6 days a week.
   - So, her weekly earnings are:  
     \( 11.25 \times 6 = 67.5 \) dollars per week.

3. **Earnings for April (without bonus):**
   - There are 4 weeks in April.
   - So, her earnings for April (without bonus) are:  
     \( 67.5 \times 4 = 270 \) dollars.

4. **Attendance Bonus:**
   - She receives an additional $10 if she has complete attendance.
   - Since she had no absences in April, she gets the bonus.

5. **Total Earnings for April:**
   - Add the bonus to her base earnings:  
     \( 270 + 10 = 280 \) dollars.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{280}

Let's analyze the problem step by step:

- The serum causes the drinker to grow an extra arm every 3 days.
- It also causes an extra leg to grow every 5 days.
- We are to find the total number of **new limbs** grown after 15 days.

First, determine the number of arms grown:
- Arms grow every 3 days. So, after 15 days, the number of arms grown is the number of multiples of 3 up to 15.
- Multiples of 3 in 15 days: 3, 6, 9, 12, 15. That's 5 arms.

Next, determine the number of legs grown:
- Legs grow every 5 days. So, after 15 days, the number of legs grown is the number of multiples of 5 up to 15.
- Multiples of 5 in 15 days: 5, 10, 15. That's 3 legs.

Now, total new limbs = new arms + new legs = 5 + 3 = 8.

Therefore, after 15 days, the serum will cause a person to grow **8 new limbs**.

   - Therefore, the weight per serving is \( \frac{300\, \text{g}}{5} = 60\, \text{g per serving} \).
   - So, calories per gram = \( \frac{250\, \text{calories}}{60\, \text{g}} = \frac{25}{6} \approx 4.1667\, \text{calories per gram} \).

2. **Calculate remaining calories for the day:**
   - Daily calorie target: 2000 calories
   - Already consumed: 1800 calories
   - Remaining calories: \( 2000 - 1800 = 200\, \text{calories} \).

3. **Determine how many grams of chips you can eat with the remaining calories:**
   - Let \( x \) be the grams of chips you can eat.
   - Calories from chips = \( \left( \frac{25}{6} \right) x \).
   - Set this equal to the remaining calories:
     \[
     \left( \frac{25}{6} \right) x = 200
     \]
   - Solve for \( x \):
     \[
     x = 200 \times \frac{6}{25} = \frac{1200}{25} = 48
     \]

So, you can eat **48 grams** of chips.

**Final Answer:**

Token usage: 56 input + 182 output = 238 total
Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's calculate the net pay for each job and then compare them.

**Job A:**
- Hourly wage: $15
- Hours per year: 2000
- Gross income: \(15 \times 2000 = 30,000\)
- Tax rate: 20%
- Net income: \(30,000 \times (1 - 0.20) = 30,000 \times 0.80 = 24,000\)

**Job B:**
- Gross income: $42,000
- Property tax: $6,000 (this is an additional tax, not deductible from income)
- Tax rate: 10% on net income after property tax? Wait, let's read carefully: "10% tax rate on net income after property tax". This might be ambiguous. Actually, it says "net income after property tax", which probably means that the income tax is applied after subtracting the property tax from gross income. But property tax is typically not subtracted from income for income tax purposes; it's a separate tax. However, the problem states: "charges $6,000 in property tax and a 10% tax rate on net income after property tax". This suggests that the state has two taxes: a fixed property tax of $6,000 and an income tax of 10% applied to income after subtracting the property tax? That doesn't make sense because property tax is not usually subtracted from income. Alternatively, it might mean that the income tax is 10%, and there is an additional property tax of $6,000. But the phrase "on net income after property tax" is confusing.

Let me parse the sentence: "Job B pays $42,000 a year and is in a state that charges $6,000 in property tax and a 10% tax rate on net income after property tax."

This likely means:
- There is a property tax of $6,000 (which Nick has to pay, regardless of income).
- The income tax is 10%, and it is applied to the income after subtracting the property tax? That would be unusual, but let's assume that for the purpose of this problem.

So, for Job B:
Gross income = $42,000
First, subtract property tax: \(42,000 - 6,000 = 36,000\). This is the "net income after property tax" on which the 10% income tax is applied.
Income tax = 10% of 36,000 = 0.10 * 36,000 = 3,600.
Then, total taxes = property tax + income tax = 6,000 + 3,600 = 9,600.
Net income = gross income - total taxes = 42,000 - 9,600 = 32,400.

Alternatively, if the property tax is not subtracted from income for tax calculation, but is just an additional tax:
Then, income tax = 10% of 42,000 = 4,200, and property tax = 6,000, total tax = 10,200, net = 42,000 - 10,200 = 31,800.

But the problem says "10% tax rate on net income after property tax", which suggests that the income tax base is (gross income - property tax). So the first interpretation is probably intended.

Therefore, for Job B:
Net income = Gross income - property tax - income tax on (gross - property tax)
= 42,000 - 6,000 - [0.10 * (42,000 - 6,000)]
= 36,000 - 0.10*36,000
= 36,000 - 3,600
= 32,400

So net income for Job B is $32,400.

Now, Job A net income is $24,000.

Therefore, Job B has higher net pay by: 32,400 - 24,000 = 8,400.

So, Nick will make $8,400 more at Job B.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{8400}
\]

     \text{Net change} = \text{Holes filled by Nate} - \text{Holes dug by dog} = 9 - 6 = 3 \text{ holes filled per day}
     \]
   This means that each day, the total number of holes decreases by 3.

3. **Time to Fill All Holes:**
   - Initially, there are 84 holes.
   - Since the net decrease is 3 holes per day, the number of days required to fill all holes is:
     \[
     \text{Days} = \frac{84}{3} = 28 \text{ days}
     \]

4. **Convert Days to Weeks:**
   - There are 7 days in a week, so:
     \[
     \text{Weeks} = \frac{28}{7} = 4 \text{ weeks}
     \]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{4}
\]
It takes Nate 4 weeks to fill in all the holes.
Token usage: 71 input + 359 output = 430 total
Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's denote:
- \( A \) = number of Pokemon Alex caught
- \( S \) = number of Pokemon Stan caught
- \( D \) = number of Pokemon Adelwolfe caught

We are given:
1. Together they have caught 339 Pokemon:
   \[
   A + S + D = 339
   \]
2. Alex has caught 5 more than Stan:
   \[
   A = S + 5
   \]
3. Stan has caught 13 less than 4 times as many as Adelwolfe:
   \[
   S = 4D - 13
   \]

We need to find \( S \).

Let's express all variables in terms of \( S \).

From equation 2:
\[
A = S + 5
\]

From equation 3:
\[
S = 4D - 13 \implies 4D = S + 13 \implies D = \frac{S + 13}{4}
\]

Now substitute \( A \) and \( D \) into equation 1:
\[
A + S + D = 339
\]
\[
(S + 5) + S + \frac{S + 13}{4} = 339
\]

Combine like terms:
\[
2S + 5 + \frac{S + 13}{4} = 339
\]

To eliminate the fraction, multiply every term by 4:
\[
4(2S) + 4(5) + 4 \cdot \frac{S + 13}{4} = 4 \cdot 339
\]
\[
8S + 20 + (S + 13) = 1356
\]

Combine like terms:
\[
8S + S + 20 + 13 = 1356
\]
\[
9S + 33 = 1356
\]

Subtract 33 from both sides:
\[
9S = 1356 - 33
\]
\[
9S = 1323
\]

Divide both sides by 9:
\[
S = \frac{1323}{9} = 147
\]

So, Stan has caught 147 Pokemon.

**Verification:**
- \( S = 147 \)
- \( A = S + 5 = 152 \)
- \( D = \frac{S + 13}{4} = \frac{147 + 13}{4} = \frac{160}{4} = 40 \)
- Total: \( 152 + 147 + 40 = 339 \) ✅

Thus, the answer is:
\[
\boxed{147}
\]

- Koalas: 5
- Zebras: 2
- Frogs: 4

Total current animals = \(3 + 5 + 2 + 4 = 14\)

**Step 2: Let \(g\) be the number of goats to buy.**
After buying \(g\) goats, the total number of animals becomes \(14 + g\).

**Step 3: We want goats to be 30% of the total.**
So, the number of goats (\(g\)) should be 30% of the total (\(14 + g\)):
\[
g = 0.30 \times (14 + g)
\]

**Step 4: Solve for \(g\).**
\[
g = 0.30(14 + g)
\]
\[
g = 4.2 + 0.30g
\]
\[
g - 0.30g = 4.2
\]
\[
0.70g = 4.2
\]
\[
g = \frac{4.2}{0.70} = 6
\]

**Step 5: Verify.**
After buying 6 goats:
- Total animals = \(14 + 6 = 20\)
- Goats = 6
- Percentage = \(\frac{6}{20} \times 100\% = 30\%\)

This matches the requirement.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{6}
\]
Bubbles should buy 6 stuffed goats.

Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given:**
- The plane travels 1200 miles in 3 hours.
- We need to find out how many additional hours it would take to travel an additional 2000 miles at the same rate.

**Step 1: Find the speed of the plane.**
\[
\text{Speed} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Time}} = \frac{1200 \text{ miles}}{3 \text{ hours}} = 400 \text{ miles per hour (mph)}
\]

**Step 2: Determine the time required to travel the additional 2000 miles.**
\[
\text{Time} = \frac{\text{Distance}}{\text{Speed}} = \frac{2000 \text{ miles}}{400 \text{ mph}} = 5 \text{ hours}
\]

**Step 3: Since the question asks for the additional hours beyond the initial 3 hours, but note that the additional distance is 2000 miles (which is entirely separate from the initial 1200 miles). So, the time for the additional 2000 miles is exactly 5 hours. There is no need to subtract the initial time because the question is specifically asking for the time to travel the additional distance.**

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{5}
\]

It would take **5 additional hours** to travel an additional 2000 miles.

   - Let \( b_j \) be the number of bananas Jamal has.
   - Then, Andrea has \( b_a = \frac{1}{2} b_j \) bananas.

\]

**From point 2:**
\[

\]

**Now, total fruits:**
- Jamal's fruits: \( a_j + b_j = 44 + 48 = 92 \)
- Andrea's fruits: \( a_a + b_a = 52 + 24 = 76 \)
- Total: \( 92 + 76 = 168 \)

- One angle is twice the smallest angle: \( 2x \)
- One angle is three times the smallest angle: \( 3x \)

Since the sum of the angles in a triangle is 180 degrees, we can write the equation:
\[
x + 2x + 3x = 180
\]

Combine like terms:
\[
6x = 180
\]

Solve for \( x \):
\[
x = \frac{180}{6} = 30
\]

Now, the largest angle is \( 3x \):
\[
3x = 3 \times 30 = 90
\]

Therefore, the measure of the largest angle is \( \boxed{90} \) degrees.

Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given:**
- The cat is 5 times faster than the turtle.
- The cat's speed is 15 feet per second.
- We need to find how many feet the turtle can crawl in 40 seconds.

**Step 1: Find the turtle's speed.**
Since the cat is 5 times faster than the turtle, the turtle's speed is:
\[
\text{Turtle's speed} = \frac{\text{Cat's speed}}{5} = \frac{15}{5} = 3 \text{ feet per second}
\]

**Step 2: Calculate the distance the turtle crawls in 40 seconds.**
Distance is given by:
\[
\text{Distance} = \text{Speed} \times \text{Time}
\]
So,
\[
\text{Distance} = 3 \text{ ft/s} \times 40 \text{ s} = 120 \text{ feet}
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{120}
\]

3. **Let’s Define**:
   - Let \( x \) be the number of birds attracted by each store-bought feeder per day.
   - Then, each self-built feeder attracts \( x + 10 \) birds per day.

4. **Total Birds**:
   - From 3 store-bought feeders: \( 3x \)
   - From 3 self-built feeders: \( 3(x + 10) = 3x + 30 \)
   - Total birds: \( 3x + 3x + 30 = 6x + 30 \)

   - This is the average before noticing the difference. So, the average per feeder is 20 birds.
   - Therefore, total birds based on average: \( 6 \times 20 = 120 \).

   \[

   \]
   Solve for \( x \):
   \[
   6x = 120 - 30 = 90
   \]
   \[
   x = 15
   \]

7. **Now, plug back to find total birds with the difference**:
   - Actually, we already have the total: \( 6x + 30 = 6(15) + 30 = 90 + 30 = 120 \).

So the total is actually higher than the initial average would suggest? But no, because the initial average is based on the actual total divided by 6.

Actually, the problem says: "each bird feeder seems to attract 20 birds throughout the day until Lillian notices..." This implies that initially she thought each attracted 20 on average, but then she realizes that the self-built ones attract more. But the total number of birds is fixed. So the total is indeed 120.

- In May, 40 employees were hired.
- Each employee is paid $15 per hour for a 40-hour workweek.
- There are 4 weeks in May (assuming a typical month with 4 weeks for simplicity, as weeks are not specified otherwise).
- In June, 1/4 of the employees' contracts expired. So, only 3/4 of the original employees are still working in June.
- Similarly, June has 4 weeks.

**Step 1: Calculate the total hours worked per employee per month.**
- Each week: 40 hours
- For 4 weeks: \(40 \times 4 = 160\) hours per month per employee.

**Step 2: Calculate the payment per employee per month.**
- Hourly wage: $15
- Monthly payment per employee: \(160 \times 15 = \$2400\)

**Step 3: Calculate total payment for May.**
- Number of employees in May: 40
- Total payment for May: \(40 \times 2400 = \$96,000\)

**Step 4: Calculate number of employees in June.**
- 1/4 of the contracts expired, so number of employees left: \(40 \times \frac{3}{4} = 30\)

**Step 5: Calculate total payment for June.**
- Number of employees in June: 30
- Total payment for June: \(30 \times 2400 = \$72,000\)

**Step 6: Total payment for both months.**
- Total = May payment + June payment = \(96,000 + 72,000 = \$168,000\)

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{168000}
\]
The company paid a total of \$168,000 to the employees in May and June.

Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Speed of the fast car**:  
   \( v_{\text{fast}} = 60 \) miles/hour.

2. **Speed of the slow car**:  
   It is traveling at half the speed of the fast car.  
   \( v_{\text{slow}} = \frac{1}{2} \times 60 = 30 \) miles/hour.

3. **Distance to cover**:  
   Both cars cover the same distance, which is 480 miles.

4. **Time taken by the slow car**:  
   Time is given by the formula:  
   \( \text{time} = \frac{\text{distance}}{\text{speed}} \).  
   So, for the slow car:  
   \( t_{\text{slow}} = \frac{480}{30} = 16 \) hours.

**Final Answer**:  
\[
\boxed{16}
\]
The slow car took 16 hours to cover 480 miles.

     \( \frac{180}{30} = 6 \).

However, let's consider the timing carefully:
- At time 0 (start), he drinks the first bottle.
- After 30 minutes, he drinks the second.
- After 60 minutes, the third.
- After 90 minutes, the fourth.
- After 120 minutes, the fifth.
- After 150 minutes, the sixth.
- At 180 minutes (end), he might drink another? But the problem says "every half hour," which typically includes the start and then every 30 minutes thereafter.

Since 180 minutes is exactly 6 half-hour intervals (from 0 to 180), he drinks at times: 0, 30, 60, 90, 120, 150. That is 6 bottles. He does not drink at 180 because that is the end time and the next half-hour would be beyond.

So, the number of bottles is 6.

\[

Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given:**
- Liam is currently 16 years old.
- Two years ago, Liam's age was twice Vince's age at that time.
- We need to find Vince's current age.

**Let:**
- Let \( V \) be Vince's current age.

**Two years ago:**
- Liam's age: \( 16 - 2 = 14 \)
- Vince's age: \( V - 2 \)

According to the problem, two years ago, Liam's age was twice Vince's age:
\[
14 = 2 \times (V - 2)
\]

**Solve for \( V \):**
\[
14 = 2(V - 2)
\]
Divide both sides by 2:
\[
7 = V - 2
\]
Add 2 to both sides:
\[
V = 9
\]

**Therefore, Vince is currently 9 years old.**

**Verification:**
- Two years ago, Liam was 14 and Vince was \( 9 - 2 = 7 \).
- Indeed, 14 is twice 7 (\( 2 \times 7 = 14 \)), which matches the condition.

Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Determine the total amount of soup:**
   - The pot can fill 4 adult bowls or 8 child bowls.
   - This implies that an adult bowl is twice the size of a child bowl. Let the size of a child bowl be \( c \). Then an adult bowl is \( 2c \).
   - Therefore, the total soup in the pot is \( 4 \times 2c = 8c \) (or equivalently, \( 8 \times c = 8c \)).

2. **Who is eating at dinner?**
   - Calvin (adult), his wife (adult), and their two children (children).
   - So, there are 2 adults and 2 children.

3. **Soup consumed at dinner:**
   - Each adult eats one adult bowl: \( 2 \times 2c = 4c \).
   - Each child eats one child bowl: \( 2 \times c = 2c \).
   - Total consumed at dinner: \( 4c + 2c = 6c \).

4. **Leftover soup:**
   - Total soup: \( 8c \).
   - Consumed: \( 6c \).
   - Leftover: \( 8c - 6c = 2c \).

5. **How many child bowls can be made from leftover?**
   - Each child bowl is \( c \).
   - So, from \( 2c \), we can make \( 2 \) child bowls.

6. **How many times can each child have a bowl for lunch?**
   - There are 2 children.
   - With 2 child bowls, each child can have \( \frac{2}{2} = 1 \) bowl.

Therefore, each child will be able to have a bowl of soup for lunch **once** from the leftover soup.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{1}
\]
Each child can have one bowl of soup for lunch from the leftovers.

Let's solve the problem step by step.

Let:
- \( x \) = my age

From the problem:
- I am three years younger than my brother:  
  Brother's age = \( x + 3 \)
- I am two years older than my sister:  
  Sister's age = \( x - 2 \)
- My mom's age is one less than three times my brother's age:  
  Mom's age = \( 3 \times (x + 3) - 1 = 3x + 9 - 1 = 3x + 8 \)

The sum of all ages is 87:
\[
x + (x + 3) + (x - 2) + (3x + 8) = 87
\]

Combine like terms:
\[
x + x + 3 + x - 2 + 3x + 8 = 87
\]
\[
(x + x + x + 3x) + (3 - 2 + 8) = 87
\]
\[
6x + 9 = 87
\]

Subtract 9 from both sides:
\[
6x = 78
\]

Divide both sides by 6:
\[
x = 13
\]

So, I am 13 years old.

**Verification:**
- My age: 13
- Brother: 13 + 3 = 16
- Sister: 13 - 2 = 11
- Mom: 3*16 - 1 = 48 - 1 = 47
Sum: 13 + 16 + 11 + 47 = 87. Correct.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{13}
\]

Token usage: 71 input + 183 output = 254 total
Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given:**
- John has twice as many candies as Robert.
- John has 54 candies.
- James has 6 more candies than Robert.

**We need to find:** How many more candies does John have than James?

**Step 1: Find the number of candies Robert has.**
Since John has twice as many as Robert:
\[
\text{John's candies} = 2 \times \text{Robert's candies}
\]
\[
54 = 2 \times \text{Robert's candies}
\]
\[
\text{Robert's candies} = \frac{54}{2} = 27
\]

**Step 2: Find the number of candies James has.**
James has 6 more than Robert:
\[
\text{James's candies} = \text{Robert's candies} + 6 = 27 + 6 = 33
\]

**Step 3: Find how many more candies John has than James.**
\[
\text{John's candies} - \text{James's candies} = 54 - 33 = 21
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{21}
\]
John has 21 more candies than James.

2. **Cookies for her son**:
   - She puts 4 cookies in his lunch box each day for 5 days a week.
   - So, cookies for son = 4 cookies/day × 5 days = 20 cookies.

3. **Cookies for her husband**:
   - He eats 1 cookie per day for 7 days.
   - So, cookies for husband = 1 cookie/day × 7 days = 7 cookies.

4. **Cookies eaten by Jenny**:
   - Total cookies = 36
   - Subtract cookies for son: 36 - 20 = 16
   - Subtract cookies for husband: 16 - 7 = 9

Therefore, Jenny eats **9 cookies** per week.

Token usage: 37 input + 103 output = 140 total
Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Initial Setup:**
   - The chalk starts at 5 inches on Monday.
   - On Monday, instead of using 20%, he uses 45% of the chalk. So, the amount used is \(0.45 \times 5 = 2.25\) inches.
   - Therefore, the remaining chalk at the end of Monday is \(5 - 2.25 = 2.75\) inches.

2. **Subsequent Days:**
   - From Tuesday onward, he uses only 20% of the **remaining** chalk each day.
   - However, he recycles the chalk when it is smaller than 2 inches.

3. **We need to find how many days (after Monday) he can use the chalk before it becomes less than 2 inches.**
   - Let \(r_n\) be the remaining chalk at the end of day \(n\) (where \(n=0\) is Monday).
   - So, \(r_0 = 2.75\) inches.
   - Each day (starting Tuesday), he uses 20% of the current chalk, so the remaining is 80% of the previous day's remaining.
   - Therefore, after \(d\) days (after Monday), the remaining chalk is:
     \[
     r_d = r_0 \times (0.8)^d
     \]
   - We want the largest integer \(d\) such that \(r_d \geq 2\).

4. **Set up the inequality:**
   \[
   2.75 \times (0.8)^d \geq 2
   \]
   Divide both sides by 2.75:
   \[
   (0.8)^d \geq \frac{2}{2.75} = \frac{8}{11} \approx 0.72727
   \]

5. **Solve for \(d\):**
   - Take the logarithm (base 0.8) of both sides. Since 0.8 < 1, the inequality reverses when taking logs:
   \[
   d \leq \log_{0.8} \left( \frac{8}{11} \right)
   \]
   - Alternatively, use natural logs:
   \[
   \ln(0.8^d) \geq \ln(8/11)
   \implies d \cdot \ln(0.8) \geq \ln(8/11)
   \]
   - Since \(\ln(0.8) < 0\), dividing by it reverses the inequality:
   \[
   d \leq \frac{\ln(8/11)}{\ln(0.8)}
   \]

6. **Calculate the value:**
   - \(\ln(8/11) = \ln(8) - \ln(11) \approx 2.07944 - 2.39790 = -0.31846\)
   - \(\ln(0.8) \approx -0.22314\)
   - So,
     \[
     d \leq \frac{-0.31846}{-0.22314} \approx 1.427
   \]
   - Since \(d\) must be an integer (number of days), we take \(d = 1\).

7. **Check for \(d=1\):**
   - After Tuesday: \(r_1 = 2.75 \times 0.8 = 2.2\) inches (which is \(\geq 2\)).
   - After Wednesday: \(r_2 = 2.2 \times 0.8 = 1.76\) inches (which is \(< 2\)).

   So, after Tuesday (1 day after Monday), the chalk is still usable (2.2 inches), but after Wednesday (2 days after Monday) it is below 2 inches and must be recycled.

8. **Therefore, he has 1 day (Tuesday) before he has to recycle the chalk.**
   - Note: The question asks: "how many days does he have before he has to recycle this piece?" This refers to the days after Monday.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{1}
\]
The teacher has **1 day** (Tuesday) before he has to recycle the chalk.

Let's break down the problem step by step:

**Step 1: Calculate the total distance run in a week.**
\[

Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's determine the picking rates for each person step by step.

1. **Tony's rate**:  
   Tony picks 6 quarts per hour.  
   So, \( T = 6 \).

2. **Bobby's rate**:  
   Bobby picks one less quart per hour than Tony.  
   So, \( B = T - 1 = 6 - 1 = 5 \).

3. **Kathy's rate**:  
   Kathy picks twice as many as Bobby.  
   So, \( K = 2 \times B = 2 \times 5 = 10 \).

4. **Ricky's rate**:  
   Ricky picks two fewer quarts per hour than Kathy.  
   So, \( R = K - 2 = 10 - 2 = 8 \).

Now, add all their rates together to find the total quarts per hour:
\[
T + B + K + R = 6 + 5 + 10 + 8
\]
Calculate the sum:
\[
6 + 5 = 11 \\
11 + 10 = 21 \\
21 + 8 = 29
\]

Therefore, together they can pick \(\boxed{29}\) quarts of strawberries per hour.

Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **James's play set has 80 toys.**
2. **Jonathan wants his play set to be five times larger than James's.**
   - So, Jonathan wants: \(5 \times 80 = 400\) toys.
3. **He currently has some number of toys, say \(x\).**
4. **He needs 2 more toys than he already has to reach 400.**
   - This means: \(x + (x + 2) = 400\)
     - Why? Because he needs "2 more than he already has" to add to his current number. So, the amount he needs is \(x + 2\), and when added to his current \(x\), it gives \(x + (x + 2) = 2x + 2\).

So, we set up the equation:
\[
2x + 2 = 400
\]

Now, solve for \(x\):
\[
2x + 2 = 400
\]
Subtract 2 from both sides:
\[
2x = 398
\]
Divide both sides by 2:
\[
x = 199
\]

**Therefore, Jonathan currently has 199 toys.**

**Verification:**
- He needs 2 more than he has: \(199 + 2 = 201\) toys to add.
- Current toys (199) + needed toys (201) = 400, which is indeed five times James's 80 toys.

\[
\boxed{199}
\]

3. **Books Purchased Today**: She bought 2 books today, each costing the same amount. Let the cost of each book be \( x \) dollars.
4. **Remaining Budget**: After all purchases, she has \$2 left.

We can set up an equation to represent the situation:

\[
\text{Total spent} + \text{Remaining budget} = \text{Total budget}
\]

The total spent includes the \$4 already spent plus the cost of the 2 books (\(2x\)):

\[
4 + 2x + 2 = 16
\]

Now, solve for \(x\):

\[
4 + 2x + 2 = 16
\]
\[
6 + 2x = 16
\]
\[
2x = 16 - 6
\]
\[
2x = 10
\]
\[
x = 5
\]

So, each book cost \$5.

**Verification**:
- Already spent: \$4
- Cost of 2 books: \(2 \times 5 = \$10\)
- Total spent: \(4 + 10 = \$14\)
- Remaining: \(16 - 14 = \$2\) (matches the given)

Thus, each book cost \(\boxed{5}\) dollars.

1. **Number of Dogs**: Mario has 6 sled dogs.

3. **Snowshoes Needed**: Since each snowshoe is for one leg, we need to find the total number of legs to determine how many snowshoes are required.
   - Total legs = Number of dogs × Legs per dog = 6 × 4 = 24 legs.
   - Therefore, 24 snowshoes are needed.
4. **Cost of Snowshoes**: Each pair of snowshoes costs $12.00. 
   - Note: A pair consists of 2 snowshoes.

   - Total cost = Number of snowshoes × Cost per snowshoe = 24 × $6.00 = $144.00.

- Since each dog has 4 legs, it requires 2 pairs of snowshoes (because 4 legs / 2 legs per pair = 2 pairs per dog).
- Pairs per dog = 2.
- Total pairs needed = 6 dogs × 2 pairs/dog = 12 pairs.
- Total cost = 12 pairs × $12.00 per pair = $144.00.

Both methods yield the same result.

\]

1. **Understand the problem:**
   - Mark was unwell for 3 months.

   - His final weight is 70 pounds.
   - We need to find his initial weight.

2. **Let the initial weight be \( x \) pounds.**
   - After the first month, his weight becomes \( x - 10 \).
   - After the second month, it becomes \( (x - 10) - 10 = x - 20 \).
   - After the third month, it becomes \( (x - 20) - 10 = x - 30 \).

3. **Set up the equation:**

   - So, \( x - 30 = 70 \).

   - \( x = 100 \)

5. **Conclusion:**
   - Mark's initial weight was 100 pounds.

Token usage: 41 input + 243 output = 284 total
Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Probability of rolling a number greater than 3:**
   - The numbers greater than 3 on a six-sided die are 4, 5, and 6.
   - So, there are 3 favorable outcomes out of 6.
   - Probability \( P(A) = \frac{3}{6} = \frac{1}{2} = 0.5 \).

2. **Probability of rolling two even numbers in a row:**
   - The even numbers on a die are 2, 4, and 6.
   - So, the probability of rolling an even number in one roll is \( \frac{3}{6} = \frac{1}{2} = 0.5 \).
   - Since the rolls are independent, the probability of two even numbers in a row is \( \left(\frac{1}{2}\right) \times \left(\frac{1}{2}\right) = \frac{1}{4} = 0.25 \).

3. **How much more likely is event A (rolling >3) than event B (two even in a row)?**
   - We want to find the percentage by which \( P(A) \) is greater than \( P(B) \).
   - The difference is \( P(A) - P(B) = 0.5 - 0.25 = 0.25 \).
   - To express this as a percentage of \( P(B) \), we compute:
        \[
        \text{Percentage more} = \left( \frac{P(A) - P(B)}{P(B)} \right) \times 100\% = \left( \frac{0.25}{0.25} \right) \times 100\% = 100\%
        \]
   - So, rolling a number greater than 3 is 100% more likely than rolling two even numbers in a row.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{100}
\]
It is 100% more likely.

Token usage: 65 input + 196 output = 261 total
Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Understand the given information:**
   - One person can paint **half a house in 5 days**.
   - We need to find out how many **hours** it would take for **5 people** to paint a **whole house**.

2. **Find the rate of one person:**
   - One person paints \( \frac{1}{2} \) house in 5 days.
   - Therefore, the rate of one person is:
     \[
     \text{Rate per person} = \frac{\frac{1}{2} \text{ house}}{5 \text{ days}} = \frac{1}{10} \text{ house per day}
     \]
   - This means one person can paint \( \frac{1}{10} \) of a house in one day.

3. **Determine the rate for 5 people:**
   - Since the work is additive, 5 people together would paint at a rate of:
     \[
     \text{Rate for 5 people} = 5 \times \frac{1}{10} = \frac{1}{2} \text{ house per day}
     \]
   - So, 5 people can paint \( \frac{1}{2} \) of a house in one day.

4. **Time to paint a whole house:**
   - To paint a whole house (which is 1 house), the time required in days is:
     \[
     \text{Time} = \frac{1 \text{ house}}{\frac{1}{2} \text{ house per day}} = 2 \text{ days}
     \]
   - Therefore, it takes 5 people **2 days** to paint the whole house.

5. **Convert days to hours:**
   - Assuming a standard workday (e.g., 8 hours per day), but the problem doesn't specify the number of hours per day. However, in such problems, we often assume that "day" refers to a full day of work, which is typically 8 hours. But let's check the context: the question asks for hours, so we need to be consistent.
   - Actually, the initial rate is given in "days", and we need to output in "hours". So we should define how many hours are in a day for this work.

   - Since it's not specified, we'll assume that a "day" of work is a standard 8-hour workday. This is a common assumption in such problems unless stated otherwise.

   - Therefore, 2 days = \( 2 \times 8 = 16 \) hours.

6. **Alternative approach (without assuming hours per day):**
   - Alternatively, we can express everything in hours from the start to avoid ambiguity.
   - One person paints \( \frac{1}{2} \) house in 5 days.
   - If we assume 8 hours per day, then 5 days = \( 5 \times 8 = 40 \) hours.
   - So, one person paints \( \frac{1}{2} \) house in 40 hours.
   - Therefore, the rate of one person is:
        \[
        \text{Rate} = \frac{\frac{1}{2}}{40} = \frac{1}{80} \text{ house per hour}
        \]
   - For 5 people:
        \[
        \text{Rate} = 5 \times \frac{1}{80} = \frac{1}{16} \text{ house per hour}
        \]
   - Time to paint one house:
        \[
        \text{Time} = \frac{1}{\frac{1}{16}} = 16 \text{ hours}
        \]

   This confirms our previous result.

7. **Conclusion:**
   - It would take **16 hours** for 5 people to paint a whole house.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{16}
\]

Let's break down the problem step by step:

1. **Total Earnings**: Tasha made a total of $80 from selling lemonade and mowing lawns.

   - First week: She mowed Kamala's lawn **three times** as often as she mowed Joe's lawn.
   - Second week: She mowed Alba's lawn **five times** as often as she mowed Joe's lawn.
   - Joe paid $6 for each time she mowed his lawn.

3. **Let’s Define Variables**:
   - Let \( x \) be the number of times Tasha mowed Joe's lawn.
   - Then, she mowed Kamala's lawn \( 3x \) times.
   - And she mowed Alba's lawn \( 5x \) times.

4. **Earnings from Lawn Mowing**:
   - Each time she mows Joe's lawn, she earns $6.
   - We assume that Kamala and Alba also pay $6 per mowing (since the problem doesn't specify otherwise, we assume the same rate).
   - So, earnings from Joe: \( 6x \)
   - Earnings from Kamala: \( 6 \times 3x = 18x \)
   - Earnings from Alba: \( 6 \times 5x = 30x \)

   - Total earnings from lawn mowing: \( 6x + 18x + 30x = 54x \)

5. **Earnings from Lemonade**:
   - Let \( L \) be the earnings from lemonade sales.
   - Total earnings: \( 54x + L = 80 \)

6. **Find \( x \)**:

<modification>Added a ratio calculation tool to handle ratio division problems and modified the workflow to use this tool when ratio problems are detected, falling back to LLM for other cases.</modification>
<graph>class Workflow:
    def __init__(
        self,
        name: str,
        llm_config,
        dataset: DatasetType,
        tools: list = None,
    ) -> None:
        self.name = name
        self.dataset = dataset
        self.llm = create_llm_instance(llm_config)
        self.custom = operator.Custom(self.llm)
        self.tools = tools or []

    async def __call__(self, problem: str):
        """
        Implementation of the workflow with tool selection
        """
        # Check if this is a ratio division problem
        if "ratio" in problem.lower() and ("divided" in problem.lower() or "share" in problem.lower()):
            try:
                # Extract ratio and total amount using LLM
                ratio_extraction = await self.custom(
                    input=problem, 
                    instruction="Extract the ratio numbers and total amount from this problem. Return in format 'ratio:a:b,total:amount'"
                )
                
                # Parse the extracted information
                extracted = ratio_extraction['response']
                ratio_parts = extracted.split('ratio:')[1].split(',total:')[0].split(':')
                total = float(extracted.split('total:')[1])
                
                # Use ratio tool
                result = await self.tool_execute(
                    tool_name="ratio_division",
                    ratio_parts=[int(ratio_parts[0]), int(ratio_parts[1])],
                    total_amount=total
                )
                
                if result['success']:
                    # Check if spending is mentioned
                    if "spent" in problem.lower() or "spend" in problem.lower():
                        spending_extraction = await self.custom(
                            input=problem,
                            instruction="Extract the amount spent. Return just the number."
                        )
                        spent = float(spending_extraction['response'])
                        final_amount = result['output']['shares'][0] - spent
                        return f"\\boxed{{{final_amount}}}", self.llm.get_usage_summary()["total_cost"]
                    else:
                        return f"\\boxed{{{result['output']['shares'][0]}}}", self.llm.get_usage_summary()["total_cost"]
            except:
                # Fall back to LLM if tool execution fails
                pass
        
        # Default LLM solution for other problems
        solution = await self.custom(input=problem, instruction=prompt_custom.MATH_SOLVE_PROMPT)
        return solution['response'], self.llm.get_usage_summary()["total_cost"]

    async def tool_execute(self, tool_name: str, **kwargs):
        """Execute a tool with given parameters"""
        for tool in self.tools:
            if tool["name"] == tool_name:
                try:
                    result = tool["function"](**kwargs)
                    return {"success": True, "output": result}
                except Exception as e:
                    return {"success": False, "error": str(e)}
        return {"success": False, "error": "Tool not found"}</graph>
<prompt>MATH_SOLVE_PROMPT = """
Solve the following math problem step by step. Show your reasoning clearly and provide the final answer in the format \\boxed{answer}.

Problem: {input}
"""

RATIO_EXTRACTION_PROMPT = """
Extract the ratio numbers and total amount from this problem. Return in the exact format 'ratio:a:b,total:amount' where a and b are the ratio numbers and amount is the total value being divided.

Example: "Gerald and Julia divided $100 in the ratio 3:2" should return "ratio:3:2,total:100"

Problem: {input}
"""

SPENDING_EXTRACTION_PROMPT = """
Extract the amount spent from this problem. Return just the number without any additional text.

Example: "Gerald spent $10 on a book" should return "10"

Problem: {input}
"""</prompt>
<tools>def ratio_division(ratio_parts: List[int], total_amount: float) -> dict:
    """
    Divide a total amount according to a given ratio.
    
    Args:
        ratio_parts: List of ratio parts (e.g., [3, 2] for 3:2 ratio)
        total_amount: Total amount to be divided
        
    Returns:
        dict: Contains 'shares' list with each party's share and 'unit_value'
    """
    if not ratio_parts:
        raise ValueError("Ratio parts cannot be empty")
    if any(part <= 0 for part in ratio_parts):
        raise ValueError("Ratio parts must be positive integers")
    if total_amount <= 0:
        raise ValueError("Total amount must be positive")
    
    total_parts = sum(ratio_parts)
    unit_value = total_amount / total_parts
    
    shares = [part * unit_value for part in ratio_parts]
    
    return {
        "shares": shares,
        "unit_value": unit_value,
        "total_parts": total_parts
    }

# Add ratio division tool to tools list
ratio_tool = {
    "name": "ratio_division",
    "description": "Divide a total amount according to a given ratio of parts",
    "function": ratio_division,
    "parameters": {
        "ratio_parts": {
            "type": "array",
            "items": {"type": "integer"},
            "description": "List of ratio parts (e.g., [3, 2] for 3:2 ratio)"
        },
        "total_amount": {
            "type": "number",
            "description": "Total amount to be divided"
        }
    },
    "returns": {
        "type": "object",
        "properties": {
            "shares": {
                "type": "array",
                "items": {"type": "number"},
                "description": "List of shares for each ratio part"
            },
            "unit_value": {
                "type": "number",
                "description": "Value of one ratio unit"
            },
            "total_parts": {
                "type": "number",
                "description": "Total number of ratio parts"
            }
        }
    }
}

# Add to existing tools list
new_tools.append(ratio_tool)</tools>

<modification>Added a ScEnsemble operator to generate multiple solutions and select the most frequent one, improving answer reliability through self-consistency voting. This addresses the issue where single LLM responses sometimes produce incorrect outputs (as seen in the logs where extracted outputs didn't match right answers).</modification>
<graph>class Workflow:
    def __init__(
        self,
        name: str,
        llm_config,
        dataset: DatasetType,
        tools: list = None,
    ) -> None:
        self.name = name
        self.dataset = dataset
        self.llm = create_llm_instance(llm_config)
        self.custom = operator.Custom(self.llm)
        self.sc_ensemble = operator.ScEnsemble(self.llm)

    async def __call__(self, problem: str):
        """
        Implementation of the workflow with self-consistency
        """
        # Generate multiple solutions
        solutions = []
        for _ in range(3):
            solution = await self.custom(input=problem, instruction=prompt_custom.SOLVE_PROMPT)
            solutions.append(solution['response'])
        
        # Select most frequent solution
        final_solution = await self.sc_ensemble(solutions=solutions, problem=problem)
        return final_solution['response'], self.llm.get_usage_summary()["total_cost"]</graph>

Let's solve the problem step by step:

**Problem Analysis:**
- The singer has 50 microphones.
- He wants to arrange them in pairs (each pair takes 2 microphones).
- After arranging some in pairs, 20% of the total microphones are left without space (i.e., they cannot be paired).
- We need to find how many pairs he was able to arrange.

**Let's define:**
- Let \( p \) be the number of pairs arranged.
- Each pair uses 2 microphones, so the number of microphones used in pairs is \( 2p \).
- The number of microphones left unpaired is 20% of 50, which is \( 0.2 \times 50 = 10 \).

**Total microphones:**
The total microphones are the sum of those used in pairs and those left unpaired:
\[
2p + 10 = 50
\]

**Solve for \( p \):**
\[
2p = 50 - 10
\]
\[
2p = 40
\]
\[
p = 20
\]

**Verification:**
- Number of pairs = 20, so microphones used = \( 20 \times 2 = 40 \).
- Microphones left = 10, which is 20% of 50 (since \( \frac{10}{50} = 0.2 \) or 20%).
- This matches the problem statement.

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{20}
\]

Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given:**
- Number of students' cars = 64
- Each student's car has 4 windows.
- Hannah smashes a quarter (1/4) of the students' cars' windows.

- Number of teachers' cars = 32
- Each teacher's car has 2 windows.
- Hannah smashes three-quarters (3/4) of the teachers' cars' windows.

**Step 1: Calculate the total number of windows in students' cars.**
Total windows in students' cars = Number of students' cars × Windows per car  
= 64 × 4  
= 256 windows

**Step 2: Calculate the number of windows smashed in students' cars.**
Smashed windows (students) = (1/4) × Total windows in students' cars  
= (1/4) × 256  
= 64 windows

**Step 3: Calculate the total number of windows in teachers' cars.**
Total windows in teachers' cars = Number of teachers' cars × Windows per car  
= 32 × 2  
= 64 windows

**Step 4: Calculate the number of windows smashed in teachers' cars.**
Smashed windows (teachers) = (3/4) × Total windows in teachers' cars  
= (3/4) × 64  
= 48 windows

**Step 5: Total windows smashed.**
Total smashed windows = Smashed windows (students) + Smashed windows (teachers)  
= 64 + 48  
= 112 windows

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{112}

Let's solve the problem step by step:

**Given:**
- Billy helps 2 people per hour.
- He works for 3 hours per day.
- He takes 20% of the days off between March 1st and April 19th.
- There are 31 days in March.

**Step 1: Determine the total number of days from March 1st to April 19th.**
- March has 31 days, so from March 1 to March 31 is 31 days.
- April 1 to April 19 is 19 days.
- Total days = days in March + days in April = 31 + 19 = 50 days.

**Step 2: Calculate the number of days he takes off.**
- He takes 20% of the days off.
- Days off = 20% of 50 = (20/100) * 50 = 0.2 * 50 = 10 days.

**Step 3: Calculate the number of days he works.**
- Total days = 50
- Days off = 10
- Working days = 50 - 10 = 40 days.

**Step 4: Calculate the number of people he helps per day.**
- He helps 2 people per hour.
- He works 3 hours per day.
- People helped per day = 2 * 3 = 6 people.

**Step 5: Calculate the total number of people he helps.**
- Total people helped = people per day * working days = 6 * 40 = 240 people.

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{240}

Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Problem:**
Jim spends 2 hours watching TV and then decides to go to bed and reads for half as long. He does this 3 times a week. How many hours does he spend on TV and reading in 4 weeks?

**Step 1: Analyze the problem carefully.**
- Each time Jim does this activity, he:
  - Watches TV for 2 hours.
  - Reads for half as long as TV time. So, reading time = (1/2) * 2 hours = 1 hour.
- Therefore, per session: total time = TV time + reading time = 2 + 1 = 3 hours.
- He does this 3 times a week.
- We need to find the total time over 4 weeks.

**Step 2: Break it down into smaller parts.**
- First, find the total time per week.
- Then, multiply by 4 to get the total for 4 weeks.

**Step 3: Show all calculations.**
- Time per session = TV time + reading time = 2 hours + (1/2 * 2) hours = 2 + 1 = 3 hours.
- Number of sessions per week = 3.
- Total time per week = 3 sessions * 3 hours/session = 9 hours.
- Total time in 4 weeks = 4 weeks * 9 hours/week = 36 hours.

**Step 4: Provide the final answer.**
Jim spends a total of 36 hours on TV and reading in 4 weeks.

\boxed{36}

Let's solve the problem step by step:

**Given:**
- The ball is dropped from the third-floor balcony.
- Each story is 24 feet high.
- The ball bounces to \( \frac{2}{3} \) of its previous height after each bounce.

**Step 1: Determine the starting height.**
The third floor is 3 stories above the ground. Since each story is 24 feet, the starting height is:
\[
3 \times 24 = 72 \text{ feet}
\]

**Step 2: Understand the bounces.**
- **First drop:** The ball is dropped from 72 feet. It falls to the ground (this is not a bounce; it's the initial drop).
- **First bounce:** After hitting the ground, it bounces back up to \( \frac{2}{3} \) of the starting height.
- **Second bounce:** After hitting the ground again, it bounces back up to \( \frac{2}{3} \) of the height it reached after the first bounce.

We are asked for the height after the **second bounce**.

**Step 3: Calculate the height after the first bounce.**
After the first bounce, the ball reaches:
\[
\frac{2}{3} \times 72 = 48 \text{ feet}
\]

**Step 4: Calculate the height after the second bounce.**
After the second bounce, it reaches \( \frac{2}{3} \) of the height after the first bounce:
\[
\frac{2}{3} \times 48 = 32 \text{ feet}
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{32}
\]

The ball goes to 32 feet on its second bounce.

Let's solve the problem step by step.

**Given:**
- Carly makes cupcakes and brownies.
- She makes 2 less than three times as many brownies as cupcakes.
- The class has 5 people, and each person gets 2 treats.
- We need to find the number of cupcakes made.

Let:
- \( c \) = number of cupcakes
- \( b \) = number of brownies

From the problem:
- Brownies are 2 less than three times the cupcakes: \( b = 3c - 2 \)

Total treats: cupcakes + brownies = \( c + b \)
Each of the 5 people gets 2 treats, so total treats needed = \( 5 \times 2 = 10 \)

Therefore:
\[ c + b = 10 \]

Substitute \( b \) from the first equation:
\[ c + (3c - 2) = 10 \]
\[ c + 3c - 2 = 10 \]
\[ 4c - 2 = 10 \]
\[ 4c = 10 + 2 \]
\[ 4c = 12 \]
\[ c = 3 \]

So, Carly made 3 cupcakes.

Let's verify:
Brownies: \( b = 3 \times 3 - 2 = 9 - 2 = 7 \)
Total treats: \( 3 + 7 = 10 \), which is exactly enough for 5 people each having 2 treats.

**Final Answer:**
\boxed{3}

Cost: $0.000000 ($0.000000 for input, $0.000000 for output)
Let's solve the problem step by step:

**Given:**
- Ryan's weekly allowance: $6 (for completing chores)
- Number of weeks he did chores: 3
- Cost per ice cream cone: $1.25
- Number of ice cream cones: 4 (for himself and 3 friends)
- Cost per movie ticket: $6.50

**Step 1: Calculate Ryan's total earnings from chores.**
Weekly allowance = $6  
Number of weeks = 3  
Total earnings = 6 × 3 = $18

**Step 2: Calculate the cost of the ice cream cones.**
Cost per cone = $1.25  
Number of cones = 4  
Total cost for ice cream = 1.25 × 4 = $5.00

**Step 3: Determine the remaining money after buying ice cream.**
Remaining money = Total earnings - Cost of ice cream  
= 18 - 5 = $13

**Step 4: Calculate how many movie tickets Ryan can buy with the remaining money.**
Cost per movie ticket = $6.50  
Number of tickets = Remaining money ÷ Cost per ticket  
= 13 ÷ 6.50

Let's compute this:
13 ÷ 6.50 = 1300 ÷ 650 (multiplying numerator and denominator by 100 to avoid decimals)  
= 1300 / 650 = 130 / 65 = 26 / 13 = 2

So, 13 ÷ 6.50 = 2

**Verification:**
2 tickets cost: 2 × 6.50 = $13.00 (exactly the remaining money)

**Therefore, Ryan can buy 2 movie tickets.**
